## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, Figure 1B-2, Page 7, p.7. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 1B-1, once the FHWA review results in a "Yes" at the "Approved?" decision point, the next step is that the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. From there the process branches into two parallel activities: evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ, both of which feed into the final step of providing FHWA a copy of the final report. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." (This is why installation occurs only after approval in the flowchart.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows the Figure 1B-1 flowchart where the "Yes" branch from the "Approved?" diamond leads directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then splits to "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ." [Image 2] presents the same Figure 1B-1 in its full-page context on Page 7, confirming the sequence following approval. [Image 3] shows Figure 1B-2, which addresses the separate process for incorporating devices into the MUTCD after successful experimentation, not the immediate post-approval step. [Image 4] provides text of Sections 1B.06 and 1B.07 discussing changes and interim approvals, which occur after experimentation, not immediately after approval. [Image 5] shows the Standard requirements for the contents of an experimentation request, including the semi-annual reporting schedule that appears as a downstream step in the flowchart. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Support §9 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Page 48

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.07, Section 1B.06, Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, p.9, p.11. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-2, once a change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, FHWA first develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, then publishes that Notice in the Federal Register. This is followed by a docket comment period, FHWA's review of the comments received, and FHWA's preparation of the Final Rule, after which the Final Rule is published. (In parallel, FHWA may also decide whether to grant an interim approval for the device pending completion of rulemaking.) Standards (mandatory): - After the Final Rule is published, "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-2 shows the "YES" branch from the "Accepted for Federal rulemaking?" decision flowing through: FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments → FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in Federal Register → Docket comment period → FHWA reviews comments → FHWA prepares Final Rule → FHWA publishes Final Rule, with a parallel "Interim approval?" decision branch leading to interim approval issuance and deployment while rulemaking proceeds; the flowchart also shows the post-publication requirement that State manuals be in substantial conformance within 2 years per 23 CFR 655.603(a). Support (informational): - Figure 1B-2 depicts this rulemaking sequence, including the parallel interim approval path in which "FHWA issues Interim Approval with technical conditions for use, and posts on MUTCD website," jurisdictions apply for and receive interim approval, and deploy devices under interim approval conditions until the Final Rule is issued. - "A diagram indicating the process for incorporating new traffic control devices into this Manual is shown in Figure 1B-2," and requests for change are evaluated for potential safety and operational benefits to justify inclusion in a future rulemaking. - "The issuance by FHWA of an interim approval might result in the traffic control device or application being proposed for adoption in the next scheduled rulemaking process"; if it is not proposed, a statement of the interim approval's status will be included in the Federal Register notice for the rulemaking. Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Section 1B.06 Support §4 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §4 (p.11) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

## F004

**Question:** In Figure 2A-2, what minimum mounting height and lateral offset are shown for a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a wider shoulder?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2A.16, Figure 2A-2, p.58, p.60, p.51. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a shoulder wider than 6 feet, Figure 2A-2 (Diagram A) shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way. (When a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, Diagram B instead shows a 6-foot minimum offset from the edge of the shoulder.) Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - "For post-mounted signs, the minimum lateral offset should be 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. If a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, the minimum lateral offset for post-mounted signs should be 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Diagram A ("Roadside sign in rural area") depicts a STOP sign with "5 ft MIN." mounting height above the near edge of pavement and "12 ft MIN." lateral offset, while Diagram B shows the alternative "6 ft MIN." offset that applies only where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet. [Image 2] The full page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same 5 ft MIN. height and 12 ft MIN. offset annotations for the rural roadside sign in Diagram A. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Guidance §3 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

[Image 2] and [Image 5] concern yield lines and crosswalk markings and do not bear on this dimension. Citations: - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Section 8A.06 Guidance §3 (p.989) - Page 121 - Page 93

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinguishing visual feature in Figure 2A-5 is whether the paths of the opposing left-turning vehicles cross each other within the median. In Panel A ("Separate intersections"), the divided highway has a wide median and the blue left-turn paths intersect (cross) inside the median area; in Panel B ("Single intersection"), the median is narrow and the opposing left-turn paths curve past each other without crossing. Guidance (recommended): - A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: "A. The paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and B. There is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway." - At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows Panel A labeled "Separate intersections (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross)" with a wide median where the two blue left-turn arrows overlap and cross, and Panel B labeled "Single intersection (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross)" with a narrow median where the blue left-turn arrows pass without intersecting. - [Image 2] The full page view of page 64 shows the same Figure 2A-5 contrast along with the Support text explaining that divided highway crossings with median widths between 30 and 85 feet might function as one or two intersections depending on opposing left-turn path interaction. Citations: - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Figure 2A-5 (p.64) - Page 105

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 2B-1, the STOP (R1-1) sign is an octagon (red background with white legend and border), and the YIELD (R1-2) sign is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle (red with a white interior triangle containing the red YIELD legend). The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque has a white legend and border on a red background. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." - "The YIELD (R1-2) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall not be displayed using a changeable message sign." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the R1-1 STOP sign as a red octagon with white legend, the R1-2 YIELD sign as a downward-pointing triangle with a red border and white center, and the R1-3P ALL-WAY plaque as a horizontal red rectangle with white legend and border. - [Image 2] Page 115 (Section 2B.04/2B.05 with Figure 2B-1) confirms the octagonal STOP sign, triangular YIELD sign, and the red ALL-WAY plaque with white legend and border. - [Image 3] Page 1015 shows the same octagonal STOP and triangular YIELD sign shapes reproduced within school crossing signs (R1-5a, R1-5c, R1-6 series). - [Image 4] Page 835 (Figure 6G-1) again depicts the R1-1 as a red octagon and the R1-2 as a downward-pointing red-and-white triangle in temporary traffic control contexts. - [Image 5] Page 159 (Figure 2B-20) shows octagonal STOP signs in an intersection signing layout, consistent with the R1-1 shape in Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Section 2B.05 Standard §2 (p.75) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74) - Page 115

**Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

The first interval (Walk) is labeled with a minimum of 7 seconds, and the last interval (Buffer) is labeled with a minimum of 2 seconds. Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement." - "The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Options (permitted): - "The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less." The figure also notes "The Walk Interval may be reduced under some conditions (see Section 4I.06)" and that the Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, "may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." Support: - "Figure 4I-4 illustrates the pedestrian intervals and their possible relationships with associated vehicular signal phase intervals." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 depicts the sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval → Buffer Interval, with "7 seconds MIN.**" labeled under the Walk Interval and "2 seconds MIN." labeled at the Buffer Interval, and the Pedestrian Change Interval spanning the "Calculated pedestrian clearance time*** (see Section 4I.07)." [Image 2] Page 724 shows the same Figure 4I-4 in its page context along with Section 4I.06 text. **Gold answer:** Walk interval, pedestrian change interval, then buffer interval. The walk interval is labeled 7 seconds minimum and the buffer interval 2 seconds minimum, subject to the stated walk-interval exception.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 6B-1 shows the four component areas of a temporary traffic control (TTC) zone. In the order a driver encounters them, they are: (1) the advance warning area, which tells traffic what to expect ahead; (2) the transition area, which moves traffic out of its normal path; (3) the activity area, where the work takes place (including the work space, traffic space, and buffer spaces); and (4) the termination area, which lets traffic resume normal operations. This division into four areas is informational (Support), applied as needed based on engineering judgment. Standards (mandatory): - None in the provided evidence; the four-area division is described as Support, not a mandatory requirement. Guidance (recommended): - None in the provided evidence. Options (permitted): - None relevant to the identification of the four areas (the Option provision cited below concerns substituting vehicle-mounted devices for channelizing devices in mobile operations, not the area layout itself). Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 labels the four zones from bottom (upstream) to top (downstream) as the Advance Warning Area ("tells traffic what to expect ahead"), Transition Area ("moves traffic out of its normal path"), Activity Area ("is where work takes place," containing the work space, buffer spaces, and traffic space), and Termination Area ("lets traffic resume normal operations"). [Image 2] Page 813 reproduces the same Figure 6B-1 with identical labeling of the four areas. [Image 5] Page 811 contains Section 6B.03, stating that "A TTC zone is often divided into four areas as needed... the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area," and that Figure 6B-1 illustrates them. Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772) - Page 813 - Page 811

**Gold answer:** Advance warning area, transition area, activity area, and termination area.

Support: - "The optimum method of displaying a STOP or SLOW message is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff that is tall enough that when the end of the staff is resting on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic." - "The use of paddles and flags by flaggers is illustrated in Figure 6D-1." The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The principal layout difference is the right-of-way in which the crossing lies. In Figure 8E-1, the pathway grade crossing (drawing A) sits in its own independent "Pathway right-of-way," separated from any roadway, while the sidewalk grade crossing (drawing B) runs immediately alongside the roadway within the "Highway right-of-way." Because the sidewalk is close to the roadway's traveled way, the roadway grade crossing's traffic control devices can influence or warn sidewalk users, whereas they cannot adequately do so for pathway users. This distinction is explanatory (Support), not a normative requirement. Standards (mandatory): - Although not the layout difference itself, both crossing types share this requirement: "Detectable warnings (see Chapter 3C) shall be used at pathway grade crossings where pedestrian travel is permitted and at sidewalk grade crossings and shall extend across the full width of the pathway or sidewalk." Figure 8E-1 shows detectable warnings on both approaches in both drawings. Support (informational): - A pathway is frequently placed in its own right-of-way on an alignment independent of any roadway; if built parallel to a roadway, it is physically separated by an open space or barrier such that the roadway grade crossing's traffic control devices do not exert an influence over or provide adequate warning to pathway users. - A sidewalk runs parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way and is close enough to the edge of the roadway's traveled way that the roadway grade crossing's traffic control devices can frequently exert an influence over or provide adequate warning to sidewalk users. - Pathways are typically used by both pedestrians and bicyclists, whereas sidewalks are typically used only by pedestrians. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 8E-1 shows drawing A with a standalone pathway crossing the tracks within its own "Pathway right-of-way," and drawing B with a sidewalk crossing the tracks directly adjacent to a roadway, both contained within the "Highway right-of-way." - [Image 2] The full page (p.1035) presents Section 8E.01's explanation alongside Figure 8E-1, confirming that the figure's purpose is to illustrate the pathway-versus-sidewalk right-of-way distinction. - [Image 4] Page 1077 shows Figure 8E-2, in which the sidewalk grade crossing is placed near the roadway crossing with its own flashing-light signals and detectable warnings, consistent with the sidewalk configuration in Figure 8E-1. Citations: - Section 8E.01 Support §4 (p.1035) - Section 8E.04 Standard §7 (p.1038) - Figure 8E-1 (p.1035) - Page 1076 - Page 1077

**Gold answer:** The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tracks with the roadway.

## M001

**Question:** A provision appears under a Guidance heading and uses the word 'should.' Is it mandatory, and when may a deviation be appropriate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, Section 2D.06, p.13, p.209. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, it is not mandatory. A provision under a Guidance heading using "should" is a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, not a required one. Deviations from Guidance are allowed whenever engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates the deviation to be appropriate. Standards (mandatory): - The Manual's headings shall be defined such that Guidance is "a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." By contrast, a Standard is "a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice." Guidance (recommended): - Guidance provisions use "should" language, e.g., "guide signs should be limited to no more than three lines of destinations" — such statements express recommended practice for typical situations, subject to deviation based on engineering judgment or study. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 2D.06 Guidance §2 (p.209)

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A retroreflective strip on a sign support is one of the permitted methods (based on engineering judgment) for enhancing sign conspicuity. When used, it is mandatory that the strip be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign's background color (except red for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs), and carry no legend or other information. A related mandatory application exists for Crossbuck supports at passive grade crossings, where a white strip is required on the back of the support. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." - For passive grade crossings: "A vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used on each Crossbuck support at passive grade crossings for the full length of the back of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, except as provided in Paragraph 18 of this Section." Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, where improvement of the conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, various methods may be used as appropriate (see Figure 2A-1), including a vertical retroreflective strip on the sign support. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, panel E, illustrates a "Vertical retroreflective strip on sign support" beneath a fluorescent yellow-green pedestrian warning sign assembly, with the strip color matching the sign background and a "2 ft MAX." dimension shown from the bottom of the strip to the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 2] shows the same Figure 2A-1 page (p.48) with panel E depicting the strip running the full length of the support down to within 2 feet of the roadway edge. - [Image 3] shows Section 2A.21 (p.62), which notes that information regarding the use of retroreflective material on the sign support is contained in Section 2A.11. - [Image 4] shows the Section 2A.11 text (p.49), including method L (adding a strip of retroreflective material to the sign support) and the Standard in Paragraph 5. - [Image 5] shows Figure 8B-2 (Crossbuck Assembly), depicting a "2-inch white retroreflective strip on back of support" and an "Optional 2-inch red or white retroreflective strip on front of support," ending "2 ft MAX." above the edge of the roadway. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 8B.04 Standard §17 (p.1000) - Section 2A.21 Support §8 (p.62) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48)

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area, the normal minimum mounting height is 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. The normal minimum lateral offset shown in Figure 2A-2 is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet. Where roadside features make these offsets impractical, a reduced offset of at least 2 feet may be used under Section 2A.16. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For contrast, in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, "the minimum height... of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - The figure note accompanying Section 2A.13/Figure 2A-3 states: "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way. See Section 2A.16 for lower minimums that may be used in urban areas, or where lateral offset space is limited." Options (permitted): - "On conventional, low-volume rural, and special-purpose roads in areas where it is impractical to locate a sign with the lateral offset prescribed by this Section because of roadside features such as terrain or vegetation, a lateral offset of at least 2 feet may be used." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, panel A ("Roadside sign in rural area"), shows a STOP sign with "5 ft MIN." mounting height and "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way; panel B shows "6 ft MIN." offset where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet, with the same "5 ft MIN." height. - [Image 2] Figure 2A-2 on page 51 confirms the same rural dimensions (5 ft MIN. height; 12 ft MIN. or 6 ft MIN. lateral offset) alongside the note referring to Section 2A.16 for reduced offsets. - [Image 4] Page 59 contains Section 2A.15 mounting-height provisions and the beginning of Section 2A.16 (Lateral Offset), noting that Figure 2A-2 illustrates the mounting height requirements. - [Image 5] Figure 2A-3 on page 52 carries the note that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2A.16 Option §9 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51)

**Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

(i.e., only the sanctioned Table 1D-2 abbreviations may be used for PCMS word messages — "DLY" is not among them.) Guidance (recommended): - If an approved word from Table 1D-2 is abbreviated and a prompt word is shown, the abbreviation "should not be used on a portable changeable message sign ... unless the prompt word shown in Table 1D-2 either precedes or follows the abbreviation, as applicable." - Where multiple approved abbreviations exist for a word, "the same abbreviation should be used throughout a single jurisdiction." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 1D-3 (Unacceptable Abbreviations) explicitly shows the row "DLY — Intended Word: Delay — Common Misinterpretation: Daily," confirming DLY is prohibited. Citations: - Section 1D.08 Standard §4 (p.34) - Section 2L.05 Standard §6 (p.516) - Section 1D.08 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §3 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §5 (p.34) - Table 1D-3 (p.37)

**Gold answer:** No. DLY is explicitly prohibited because it can be misinterpreted as DAILY.

Options (permitted): - "The EXCEPT RIGHT TURN (R1-10P) plaque (see Figure 2B-1) may be mounted below the STOP sign if an engineering study determines that a special combination of geometry and traffic volumes is present that makes it possible for right-turning traffic on the approach to be allowed to enter the intersection without stopping." ## M006

**Question:** Compare the quantitative triggers in the four-leg All-Way Stop crash warrant and the 8-hour volume warrant.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.13, Section 2B.16, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. | The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a p… Adjudication note: Correct crash and eight-hour volume thresholds; omits the 70-percent high-speed adjustment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.624 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Both are Option-level (permitted) All-Way Stop warrants based on an engineering study, but they use different quantitative triggers. The crash warrant for a four-leg intersection is triggered by five or more reported crashes in 12 months, or six or more in 36 months, of a type correctable by all-way stop control. The 8-hour volume warrant is triggered by combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volumes of at least 300 units per hour entering from the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour from the minor-street approaches, for each of the same 8 hours of a typical day. Options (permitted): - Crash Experience (Warrant A, four-leg): "All-way stop control may be installed at an intersection where an engineering study indicates that: A. For a four-leg intersection, there are five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control." - 8-Hour Volume (Warrant D): "All-way stop control may be installed at an intersection where an engineering study indicates: A. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day; and B. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." Key comparison points: - Metric: the crash warrant counts reported crashes of a correctable type (5 in 12 months or 6 in 36 months for four-leg intersections); the volume warrant counts multimodal entering volumes (motor vehicles, bicycles, and pedestrians combined). - Time basis: the crash warrant looks retrospectively over 12- or 36-month periods; the volume warrant requires thresholds to be met concurrently — the minor-street 200 units/hour must occur during the same 8 hours as the major-street 300 units/hour. - Both warrants require an engineering study and both are permissive ("may be installed"), not mandatory. Citations: - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a possible 70-percent adjustment if major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph.

The response identifies: Section 2A.22, Table 2A-5, p.62, p.63. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. | The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The Standard requires the agency to use an assessment or management method designed to maintain retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums; the accompanying Support text explicitly states that an agency using such a method remains in compliance even if some individual signs are below the minimums at a particular point in time. For white-on-red signs such as STOP signs, Table 2A-5 requires maintained levels of white ≥ 35 and red ≥ 7 cd/lx/m² (at 0.2° observation and −4.0° entrance angles), with a minimum sign contrast ratio of at least 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity). Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums, and "Signs that are identified through the agency’s method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude the following from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; Acknowledgment signs; and Bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. Support (explanatory): - "Compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section is achieved by having a method in place and using the method... an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, "Minimum Maintained Retroreflectivity Levels," which lists the white-on-red row (W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7 for all sheeting types) and footnote 4 requiring a minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity), the criteria applicable to STOP-type signs. Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Support §3 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The reduced ("Minimum" column) sign sizes may only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, and low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less — a multi-lane roadway posted at 35 mph does not qualify, so the full (Conventional Road / multi-lane) sizes must be used. The evidence provided does not include the specific regulatory sign size table (e.g., Table 2B-1) giving the exact STOP sign (R1-1) dimensions, so I cannot state the precise required STOP sign size from this evidence; what the evidence does establish is that a sign on this roadway shall not be smaller than the Conventional Road size, since the Minimum-column exception does not apply. Standards (mandatory): - "The sign dimensions prescribed in the sign size tables in the various Parts and Chapters in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05) shall be used unless engineering judgment determines that other sizes are appropriate in accordance with the following." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 3 of this Section, where engineering judgment determines that sizes smaller than the prescribed dimensions are appropriate for use, the sign dimensions shall not be less than the minimum dimensions specified in this Manual." - "The sizes shown in the Minimum columns that are smaller than the sizes shown in the Conventional Road columns in the various sign size tables in this Manual shall only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, and on low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; and only where the reduced legend size would be adequate for the regulation or warning or where physical conditions preclude the use of larger sizes." (A 35 mph multi-lane roadway exceeds this 30 mph threshold, so reduced sizes are prohibited.) Guidance (recommended): - For guide-sign lettering, Table 2D-2 separates Single-Lane and Multi-Lane columns by speed range (Less than 30 mph, 30–40 mph, Greater than 40 mph); on a multi-lane 35 mph roadway the Multi-Lane 30–40 mph column applies, not the Single-Lane column. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2D-2 shows distinct Single-Lane and Multi-Lane letter/numeral size columns for post-mounted guide signs, with the Multi-Lane 30–40 mph values generally larger than the Single-Lane values, confirming that single-lane sizes are not the applicable values on a multi-lane 35 mph roadway. - [Image 2] Table 7B-1's notes state that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column, illustrating that Minimum-column reductions are not available on multi-lane roads. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.07 Guidance §5 (p.210) - Table 2D-2 (p.210) - Table 7B-1 (p.971) Note on the STOP sign size: the specific dimensional table for the STOP sign (R1-1) is not included in the evidence provided, so the exact required dimension (e.g., a specific width x height) cannot be quoted here; the evidence supports only that the full Conventional Road size — not a reduced Minimum size — must be used on this roadway.

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a potential stop condition (Condition B, deceleration to 0 mph — the column keyed to Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs), Table 2C-3 shows an advance placement distance of 495 feet for a 55-mph posted or 85th-percentile speed. No — the distance is not an inflexible mandate: the table is expressly titled "Guidelines," and Section 2C.04 (Guidance) states the distances should be applied with engineering judgment. Standards (mandatory): - "The Stop Ahead (W3-1), Yield Ahead (W3-2), and Signal Ahead (W3-3) Advance Traffic Control signs (see Figure 2C-8) shall be installed on an approach to a primary traffic control device that is not visible for a sufficient distance to permit the road user to respond to the device (see Table 2C-3)." - At passive grade crossings with a YIELD or STOP sign: "The Yield Ahead or Stop Ahead sign shall be located in accordance with Table 2C-3. The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - Table 2C-3 ("Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs") lists 495 ft for the 55-mph row under Condition B, deceleration to 0 mph — the "potential stop" condition (footnote 3: Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs, based on stopping sight distance with a PRT of 2.5 seconds and a deceleration rate of 11.2 ft/s²). - Per the table's footnote 1, for warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility. - Section 2C.04 frames the table as guidance to be evaluated and applied with judgment: "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows the 55 mph row with 495 ft in the "0³" (potential stop) column under Condition B, and footnote 3 explaining the potential-stop basis. - [Image 2] The full page of Section 2C.04 shows the Guidance provisions that warning signs "should be placed so that they provide an adequate PRT" and that "the distances contained in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment," confirming the distances are recommended, not mandatory. - [Image 3] Figure 2C-13 (Sheet 5) directs users to "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance," illustrating the table's role as a placement aid. - [Image 4] Figure 2C-13 (Sheet 1) similarly annotates the sign location with "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance." - [Image 5] Figure 2C-2 notes "See Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines," reinforcing the table's guideline (not mandatory) character. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001) - Page 193 - Page 230 - Page 226 - Page 197

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

Guidance (recommended): - The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that, for each of any 8 hours of an average day, the vehicles per hour given in both of the 100 percent columns of Condition A in Table 4C-1 exist on the major street and the more critical minor-street approach (or, alternatively, Condition B is met at the 100 percent level). - Alternatively, the need for a signal should be considered if both Condition A and Condition B are met at their 80 percent column levels for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Options (permitted): - For warrants requiring conditions to be present for a certain number of hours, any four consecutive 15-minute periods may be considered as 1 hour, provided the separate 1-hour periods do not overlap and both major-street and minor-street volumes are for the same specific 1-hour periods. Meeting the warrant does not compel installation; an engineering study must determine whether a signal is justified.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under the one-year application of Warrant 7 (Crash Experience), a four-leg intersection satisfies the crash condition if the number of reported angle and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the applicable threshold in Table 4C-2 — either for total angle and pedestrian crashes (all severities) or for total fatal-and-injury angle and pedestrian crashes. The specific numeric threshold values from Table 4C-2 are not included in the evidence provided. In addition to the crash threshold, two other categories of conditions must also be met: (A) an adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce crash frequency, and (C) a traffic volume condition applying to each of any 8 hours of an average day (the volume text is truncated in the evidence). Guidance (recommended): - The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that all of the following criteria are met: "A. Adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce the crash frequency." - For the one-year crash condition: "1. The number of reported angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total angle crashes and pedestrian crashes (all severities); or 2. The number of reported fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes." - A third criterion (C) applies "For each of any 8 hours of an average day..." — this is a traffic volume condition, but the provision is truncated in the evidence and its full requirement cannot be quoted. Options (permitted): - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, or if the intersection lies within the built-up area of an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000, the 56 percent volume columns in Table 4C-1 may be used in place of the 80 percent columns, and Tables 4C-4 and 4C-5 may be used in place of Tables 4C-2 and 4C-3 (Table 4C-4 provides the reduced one-year crash thresholds, including a Four Legs column). - Agencies may calibrate HSM safety performance functions to their own crash data or develop their own SPFs, and when documented in the engineering study, these agency-specific crash frequency values may be used instead of the values in Tables 4C-2 through 4C-5. The Section 2B.11 crash figures (e.g., three crashes in 12 months for a four-leg intersection) pertain to minor-road stop control, not the Crash Experience signal warrant, and are not applicable to this question.

**Gold answer:** The thresholds are at least 5 all-severity angle/pedestrian crashes or at least 3 fatal-and-injury angle/pedestrian crashes. In addition, an adequate trial of alternatives must have failed, and the applicable 80-percent vehicular-volume or pedestrian-volume condition must be met for the required hours.

**Gold answer:** They should be continuously visible from at least 460 feet in advance of the stop line. If at least two faces are not continuously visible for that distance, a warning sign should be installed for approaching traffic.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Section 4I.06 and Figure 4I-4, the pedestrian intervals occur in this order: a Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, 7 seconds minimum), followed immediately by a Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, with optional countdown for intervals of 7 seconds or less), followed by a Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) of at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released. The pedestrian clearance time is normally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, with slower speeds considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement. The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." [shown on Page 767] - Per the Figure 4I-4 footnote: "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. Options (permitted): - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval; the countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence — steady hand, then Walk Interval (7 seconds MIN.), then Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing hand with countdown, spanning the calculated pedestrian clearance time), then Buffer Interval (steady hand, 2 seconds MIN.) — and five possible relationships to the vehicular yellow/red intervals. - [Image 2] Page 765 shows the same Figure 4I-4 in context with Section 4I.06 text, including the 3.5 ft/s clearance-time guidance and the "Zero" point of the countdown display at the start of the buffer interval. - [Image 4] Page 767 contains the Standard requiring the pedestrian change interval to begin immediately after WALK and the buffer interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) to be displayed for at least 2 seconds before release of conflicting traffic. - [Image 3] Page 764 provides context on the up-to-4 ft/s option with extended push button press and adding extended-press time to either the walk or change interval. - [Image 5] Page 771 (Section 4J.03, pedestrian hybrid beacons) is not directly relevant to the Section 4I.06 interval sequence. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 767 - Page 765 - Page 764

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." - "Except as otherwise provided in this Section, a pedestrian hybrid beacon shall meet the provisions of Chapters 4D through 4G, 4I, and 4J." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 shows the six-stage sequence (1. Dark Until Activated; 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation; 3. Steady Yellow; 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval; 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval; 6. Dark Again Until Activated), with a legend (SY, FY, SR, FR) and the note on optional intervals. - [Image 2] Page 770 (containing Section 4J.03) states the Standard that beacon indications shall be dark between actuations and prescribes the full flashing yellow → steady yellow → steady red (walk interval) → alternating flashing red (change interval) sequence. - [Image 4] Page 769 (Section 4J.02) confirms the three-section face design with the circular yellow centered below two horizontally-aligned circular red indications referenced in Figure 4J-3. **Gold answer:** Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Section 1C.03, Section 5A.02, Table 5A-1, page 760, p.760. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. | An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: An Automated Driving System (ADS) is defined as hardware and software capable of performing the *entire* Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, a term reserved for Level 3, 4, or 5 systems. Level 2 does not qualify because, although it executes both steering and acceleration/deceleration, the human driver is still expected to perform "all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — so the system performs only part of the DDT and is categorized as ADAS. Level 3 qualifies because the system performs "all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD," with the human driver serving only as a fallback who responds to a request to intervene. Standards (mandatory): - The acronym meanings are mandatory: "ADS-Automated Driving System" and "ADAS-Advanced Driver Assistance Systems" are among the acronyms that "when used in this Manual, shall have the following meanings." Support (informational): - "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." - Per Table 5A-1, Level 2 is "the driving mode specific execution by one or more sustained driver assistance systems of both steering and acceleration/deceleration … with the expectation that the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task," and its Automation Category is "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)." - Per Table 5A-1, Level 3 is "the driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," and its Automation Category is "Automated Driving System (ADS)." - Background context: driving automation technology "automates some or all aspects of the driving tasks to assist or replace the human driver and can include driver assistance technology generally known as advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS)." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, where Levels 1–2 are grouped under the "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" category while Levels 3–5 are grouped under "Automated Driving System (ADS)," visually confirming the categorical split at Level 3. [Image 4] shows page 760 with both the Section 5A.03 definition of ADS ("used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system") and Table 5A-1 immediately below it. Citations: - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31) - Section 5A.02 Support §1 (p.759) - Page 800

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the direction of travel, a TTC zone is typically divided into four areas: the advance warning area (tells traffic what to expect ahead), the transition area (moves traffic out of its normal path), the activity area (where work takes place), and the termination area (lets traffic resume normal operations). The activity area itself is comprised of three components: the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space. All of this material is explanatory (Support); no Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions were provided. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 shows the four areas in travel order—Advance Warning Area, Transition Area (with shoulder taper), Activity Area (containing the work space, traffic space, and lateral/longitudinal buffer spaces), and Termination Area (with downstream taper). [Image 2] Page 814 contains Section 6B.06, which explains that the activity area is comprised of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space, and describes each. [Image 3] Page 812 contains Section 6B.03, which lists the four component areas of a TTC zone and references Figure 6B-1, plus the descriptions of the advance warning and transition areas. [Image 4] Page 817 describes tapers (shoulder and downstream tapers) that appear in the transition and termination areas of the figure. [Image 5] Page 822 discusses worker safety within the activity area and work space but does not add to the component listing. Support (informational): - "A TTC zone is often divided into four areas as needed, based on engineering judgment: the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area. Figure 6B-1 illustrates the four areas typically included in a TTC zone." - "The activity area is the section of the highway where the work activity takes place. It is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." - "The traffic space is the portion of the highway in which road users are routed through the activity area." - The safety of workers performing tasks within the work space is of equal importance to that of the public traveling through the TTC zone, since TTC zones present constantly changing conditions unexpected by road users. Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Section 6A.01 Support §5 (p.765) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772) - Page 814 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a speed of 35 mph (which is "40 mph or less"), Table 6B-4 gives L = WS²/60 = (12 × 35²)/60 = (12 × 1,225)/60 = 245 feet. From Table 6B-3, the merging taper must be at least L = 245 feet, the shifting taper at least 0.5 L ≈ 123 feet, and the shoulder taper at least 0.33 L ≈ 81 feet. Guidance (recommended): - "The appropriate taper length (L) should be determined using the criteria shown in Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4." - "A shifting taper should have a length of approximately ½ L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4)." - "If used, shoulder tapers should have a length of approximately ¹⁄3 L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4). If a shoulder is used as a travel lane, either through practice or during a TTC activity, a normal merging or shifting taper should be used." - Consistent with the underlying formula logic, "For roadways where the speed limit is less than 45 mph, the formula L = WS²/60 should be used to compute the taper length," where W is the width of the offset in feet and S is the speed in mph. Options (permitted): - A lateral buffer space may be used to separate the traffic space from the work space (see Figures 6B-1 and 6B-2), and may also be used between two travel lanes, especially those carrying opposing flows. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 6B-4 shows the formula L = WS²/60 for speeds of 40 mph or less (and L = WS for 45 mph or more), with L = taper length in feet, W = width of offset in feet, and S = speed in mph, which yields L = 245 ft for W = 12 and S = 35. - [Image 2] Table 6B-3 lists taper length criteria: Merging Taper "at least L," Shifting Taper "at least 0.5 L," Shoulder Taper "at least 0.33 L," with a note to use Table 6B-4 to calculate L. - [Image 3] Figure 6B-2 (Types of Tapers and Buffer Spaces) illustrates the merging taper at length L, shifting tapers at ½ L, and the shoulder taper at ⅓ L, referencing Table 6B-4 for the formulas. - [Image 4] Figure 6P-39 (TA-39, Median Crossover on a Freeway) shows tapers of length L and ½ L applied in a typical layout, with a note to see Table 6B-4 for calculating taper length (L). - [Image 5] Figure 6P-38 (TA-38, Interior Lane Closure on a Freeway) shows a merging taper of length L, a ½ L shifting taper, and a shoulder taper, referencing Table 6B-4. - [Image 6] Figure 6P-42 (TA-42, Work in the Vicinity of an Exit Ramp) shows taper lengths L and shoulder tapers in typical application, again referencing Table 6B-4 for the formulas. Citations: - Section 6B.08 Guidance §4 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §8 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §10 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Support §2 (p.775) - Section 6B.06 Option §14 (p.775) - Section 3B.13 Guidance §2 (p.568) - Table 6B-4 (p.775) - Table 6B-3 (p.775) - Page 815 - Page 978 - Page 976 - Page 984

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device for flaggers because it gives road users more positive guidance than red flags (flags are reserved for emergency situations). To stop traffic, the flagger must face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward them in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally, while holding the free arm with the palm above shoulder level toward approaching traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "To stop road users, the flagger shall face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward road users in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally away from the body. The free arm shall be held with the palm of the hand above shoulder level toward approaching traffic." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." - Per Table 6G-1 (referenced in the Guidance evidence), the STOP face on the STOP/SLOW paddle (R1-1) is sized 18 x 18 inches. Support (informational): - The optimum display method is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff tall enough that, when the end of the staff rests on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6D-1 labels the STOP/SLOW paddle as the "PREFERRED METHOD" (with the flag for "EMERGENCY SITUATIONS ONLY") and depicts the "TO STOP TRAFFIC" posture — the flagger holding the 18-inch-minimum R1-1 STOP paddle on a staff with the free hand raised, palm toward traffic. [Image 2] shows the same Figure 6D-1 in its full-page context, confirming the paddle column as the preferred method and the flag column as limited to emergencies. [Image 3] shows the text of Section 6D.05, including the mandatory paddle-signaling method for stopping road users. Citations: - Section 6D.02 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6D.05 Standard §3 (p.785) - Section 6D.02 Support §5 (p.783) - Figure 6D-1 (p.784)

**Gold answer:** The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 6L-3, a Type C arrow board must have a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, a minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and a minimum of 15 elements. It must also provide three required categories of display modes: (A) a Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; (B) a flashing Double Arrow mode; and (C) a flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode. Type C boards are intended for high-speed, high-volume motor vehicle traffic control projects. Standards (mandatory): - "Arrow boards shall meet the minimum size, legibility distance, number of elements, and other specifications shown in Figure 6L-3." For Type C, Figure 6L-3 specifies: minimum size 96 x 48 inches, minimum legibility distance 1 mile, and minimum number of elements 15. - "An arrow board shall have the following three mode selections: A. A Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; B. A flashing Double Arrow mode; and C. A flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode." - "The arrow board shall have suitable elements capable of the various operating modes. The color presented by the elements shall be yellow." - "An arrow board shall be a sign with a matrix of elements capable of either flashing or sequential displays." Support (informational): - Type C arrow boards are intended to be used on high-speed, high-volume motor vehicle traffic control projects. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 6L-3, whose table lists Type C with a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, a minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and a minimum of 15 elements, and illustrates the required operating modes (Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, Sequential Chevron; Flashing Double Arrow; and Flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond Caution) using a Type C board. [Image 2] is the full page 834 view containing Figure 6L-3 with the same specifications. [Image 3] shows Section 6L.06 text on page 833, including the Standard requiring the three mode selections. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: When a reduced school speed limit zone is established, drivers approaching the zone must first encounter a School (S1-1) sign in advance, then the School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the zone begins. Where advance notice is appropriate (e.g., a speed reduction of more than 10 mph), a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign should precede the assembly. The downstream end of the zone must be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established... The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - Where higher fines apply only to speeding: "a FINES HIGHER (R2-6P), FINES DOUBLE (R2-6aP), or $XX FINE (R2-6bP) plaque shall be posted with the School Speed Limit (S5-1) sign and shall not be posted beneath the School Zone (S1-1) sign." Guidance (recommended): - "A Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign (see Figure 7B-1) should be used to inform road users of a reduced speed zone where the speed limit is being reduced by more than 10 mph, or where engineering judgment indicates that advance notice would be appropriate." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 illustrates the full sequence in each travel direction: an advance School (S1-1) sign with optional SCHOOL (S4-3P) plaque, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the start of the zone, and an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign — or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) — with an optional Speed Limit 45 (R2-1) sign at the downstream end. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 depicts the individual signs and assemblies used in the sequence, including the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P + R2-1 + S4-1P or alternative plaques), the S5-1 WHEN FLASHING sign, the S4-5/S4-5a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead signs, and the END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) and END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) signs. - [Image 3] This page presents the Section 7B.05 Standard text requiring the School Speed Limit assembly/S5-1 at the beginning of the zone, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 sign at the downstream end. - [Image 4] This page shows Section 7B.06 provisions on fines plaques posted with the School Speed Limit sign and related end-of-zone options. - [Image 5] This full-page view of Figure 7B-2 confirms the layout of the entering/leaving sign sequence for both directions of travel. - [Image 6] Figure 7B-4 shows the analogous sequence where higher fines apply only for speeding, with fines plaques included in the School Speed Limit assembly. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.06 Standard §3 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Guidance §13 (p.980) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1024 - Page 1016 - Page 1020

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.
