**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At a passive grade crossing with multiple tracks, the assembly is arranged from top to bottom as: the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque mounted directly below the Crossbuck, and the YIELD or STOP sign below the plaque on the same support, with a retroreflective strip on the support extending down to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway. The Number of Tracks plaque is required whenever two or more tracks are present at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "If a Crossbuck sign support at a passive grade crossing does not include a YIELD or STOP sign (either because the YIELD or STOP sign is placed on a separate support or because a YIELD or STOP sign is not present on the approach), a vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used for the full length of the front of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway." Guidance (recommended): - The use of STOP signs at passive grade crossings should be limited to unusual conditions where requiring all motor vehicles to make a full stop is determined to be necessary by a Diagnostic Team, considering line of sight, number of tracks, train and vehicle speeds, and crash history. - If a YIELD or STOP sign is installed on a separate support from the Crossbuck sign, it should be placed in the same plane as the Crossbuck sign and closer to the traveled way, with a minimum 2-inch separation between the nearest points of the two signs. Options (permitted): - A strip of retroreflective white material not less than 2 inches in width may be used on the back of each blade of each Crossbuck sign for the length of each blade at active grade crossings. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 8B-2 shows the vertical order on a single support — Crossbuck sign (with "3 TRACKS" plaque directly below it), then the YIELD or STOP sign, with 9 ft nominal height to the Crossbuck, minimum mounting heights of 4 ft (existing supports) or 5/7 ft (new installations), a 2-inch retroreflective strip on the support, and the strip extending to within 2 ft maximum above the roadway edge. [Image 2] Figure 8E-5 shows the same top-to-bottom arrangement (Crossbuck, "2 TRACKS" plaque, YIELD or STOP sign) applied at a pathway or sidewalk grade crossing with optional retroreflective strips on the support. [Image 5] Page 1038 repeats Figure 8E-5 with mounting-height notes for pathway/sidewalk installations. [Image 6] Page 1036 contains Section 8B.03 provisions requiring the Number of Tracks plaque below the Crossbuck when two or more tracks exist. [Image 4] Page 1080 shows Section 8B.04 Standards 11–20 on mounting heights and retroreflective strip requirements for the assembly. [Image 3] Page 1041 shows Section 8B.03/8B.04 text including the Crossbuck Assembly composition Standard and Table 8B-1 sign sizes. Citations: - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §20 (p.1000) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §6 (p.997) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §13 (p.1000) - Section 8B.03 Option §9 (p.995) - Figure 8B-2 (p.993) - Figure 8E-5 (p.1039) - Page 1041 - Page 1080 - Page 1038 - Page 1036

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Section 9E.12, a bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long (measured from the upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to the downstream edge nearest the crosswalk/intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Turns on red are prohibited from any lane where a bicycle box is placed. Green-colored pavement is optional in a bicycle box, but if it is used it must cover the full limits of the box (and bicycle boxes are among the only applications where green pavement is permitted). Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." (shown in the full-page view of Section 9E.12) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." (shown in the full-page view accompanying Figure 9E-12, Sheet 1) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: … D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes (see Section 9E.11), E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols (see Section 9E.15)." Options (permitted): - "A bicycle box (see Figure 9E-12) may be used to increase the visibility of stopped bicycles on the approach to a signalized intersection during the portion of the signal cycle when a red signal indication is being displayed to motor vehicles in the approach lane(s) that is behind the box." - "Green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1 of 2) shows a bicycle box across one lane with the "10 ft MIN." dimension between the intersection stop line and the advance stop line, bicycle symbol markings inside the box, and optional green-colored pavement filling the full box. - [Image 2] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 2 of 2) shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with a NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign, the "10 ft MIN." dimension, and required countdown pedestrian signals. - [Image 3] The full-page view of Section 9E.12 contains the Standard provisions, including "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." - [Image 4] The full-page view with Figure 9E-12 Sheet 1 contains Standard §13: "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - [Image 5] The full-page view of Section 3H.06 lists Bicycle Boxes (Section 9E.12) among the permitted applications of green-colored pavement. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Option §1 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Option §12 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Figure 9E-12 (p.1106) - Page 1146 - Page 1147 - Page 1148 - Page 666

**Gold answer:** The bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long, include at least one bicycle symbol, prohibit turns on red from its lane, and, if green pavement is used, fill the entire limits of the box.

## T001

**Question:** What is the purpose of the MUTCD, and what four objectives are used to achieve that purpose?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 U.S.C. 109(d). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. | Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility | promote consistency in use, installation, and operation Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. This purpose is achieved through four objectives: (A) promoting safety, inclusion, and mobility for all users of the road network; (B) promoting efficiency through national uniformity in the meaning and appearance of traffic control devices; (C) promoting national consistency in the use, installation, and operation of traffic control devices; and (D) providing basic principles for traffic engineers to use in decisions regarding the use, installation, operation, maintenance, and removal of traffic control devices. Note that these statements are Support (explanatory) material, not normative requirements; the related mandatory provision is that the MUTCD is the national standard for all traffic control devices. Standards (mandatory): - The MUTCD "is incorporated by reference in 23 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 655, Subpart F and shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a)." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility; promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promote consistency in use, installation, and operation; and provide basic decision-making principles for traffic engineers.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 CFR 655.603(a). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.877 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The applicability of the MUTCD to facilities open to public travel does not depend on whether the facility is publicly or privately owned, nor on its funding source. What matters is whether the facility is open to public travel: the MUTCD applies to both publicly and privately owned streets, roadways, bikeways, site roadways, and toll roads that are open to the public, and does not apply to facilities where public access is restricted at all times (or to parking areas, regardless of ownership). Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: A. Any street, roadway, or bikeway open to public travel, either publicly or privately owned; B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions... C. Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Support (informational): - "The applicability of the MUTCD to facilities open to public travel is independent of the type of ownership or jurisdiction (public or private) and the source of funding (Federal, State, local, or private)." - The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly explains (as Support material) that infrastructure elements restricting travel paths or vehicle speeds—such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces—are not traffic control devices, and that transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are likewise not traffic control devices. This is consistent with the mandatory definition, under which traffic control devices are signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message; raised islands may still be used to channelize traffic even though they are not traffic control devices. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices shall be defined as all signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices that use colors, shapes, symbols, words, sounds, or tactile information for the primary purpose of communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users on a street, highway, pedestrian facility, bikeway, pathway, or site roadway open to public travel." - The MUTCD "shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel" — but this national standard applies to traffic control devices, and the listed infrastructure elements fall outside that definition. Citations: - Section 1A.02 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 3I.01 Support §2 (p.632) - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** No. Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips, are not traffic control devices.

As supporting context, the MUTCD explains that the engineer develops the engineering-based solution, but the final decision to implement that solution rests with the agency having jurisdiction over the roadway, after consultation with and based on advice from the engineer. Standards (mandatory): - "Where the content of this Manual requires a decision for implementation, such decisions shall be made by an engineer, or an individual under the supervision of an engineer, who has the appropriate levels of experience and expertise to make the traffic control device decision." - "Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." **Gold answer:** The decision must be made by an engineer, or by an individual under the supervision of an engineer, who has the appropriate experience and expertise. The decision must use engineering judgment or an engineering study as required by the applicable MUTCD provision.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, Section 6P.01, p.13, p.857. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Adjudication note: Correct Standard, Guidance, and Option distinctions; the full Support definition is not stated. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD's four text headings signal different normative weight. A Standard is a required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice (typically using "shall"), Guidance is a recommended practice from which deviations are allowed based on engineering judgment or study (typically using "should"), and an Option is a permissive practice carrying no requirement or recommendation, sometimes modifying a Standard or Guidance statement (the "may" verb is reserved for Options, since it is excluded from Standard and Guidance statements). Note: the provided evidence is cut off before the definition of Support, so its precise definition and typical verb cannot be quoted here. Standards (mandatory): - "When used in this Manual, the text headings of Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support shall be defined as follows: A. Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. ... All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "B. Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "C. Option-a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement. All Option statements are labeled..." - "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Guidance (recommended): - (None in the evidence — the definitions themselves are stated as a Standard.) Options (permitted): - (None in the evidence.) Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857)

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Based on the evidence provided, the key documentation difference is that an engineering study must be fully documented, whereas engineering judgment carries no such explicit documentation requirement in these provisions. Deviations from Standard provisions are allowed only through a *documented* engineering study, while deviations from Guidance may rest on either engineering judgment or engineering study. Note that the formal definitions themselves reside in Section 1C.02, which is not included in the evidence, so the precise definitional wording cannot be quoted here. Standards (mandatory): - "Where the content of this Manual requires a decision for implementation, such decisions shall be made by an engineer, or an individual under the supervision of an engineer... Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." - For Standard statements: "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." - For Guidance statements: deviations are "allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate." Support (informational): - Section 1D.03 explains that when site-specific conditions make compliance with a Standard impossible or impracticable, "the deviation is allowed, provided that the agency or official having jurisdiction fully documents, through an engineering study, the engineering basis for the deviation" — underscoring that a study, not mere judgment, must be documented to justify Standard deviations. - Section 1D.03 notes that definitions of professional engineer, engineering study, and engineering judgment are provided in Section 1C.02 (not included in this evidence). - Section 4C.01 illustrates the documentation role of a study: agencies can install a signal where no warrants are met "but only after conducting an engineering study that documents the rationale." - Section 1A.01 notes the MUTCD presumes users have professional training and experience in traffic engineering when applying engineering study or judgment. Citations: - Section 1A.04 Standard §2 (p.2) - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §1 (p.32) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Section 1A.01 Support §6 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** Both involve evaluating pertinent information and applying engineering principles by a professional engineer or a person under such an engineer's supervision. Engineering judgment does not require documentation; an engineering study must be documented in writing.

The above-described examples of streets and roadways are referred to in this Manual as site roadways open to public travel." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: ... C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - Before requesting permission to experiment with a new device or application, an owner of a site roadway open to public travel should first check for any laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD that might apply. Citations: - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7)

**Gold answer:** It applies to the shopping-center site roadways that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. It does not apply to the parking area itself, including its driving aisles.

Separately, the FHWA retains authority to set other target compliance dates for particular changes. Substantial conformance of such State or other Federal agency MUTCDs or Supplements shall be as defined in 23 CFR 655.603(b)(1)." - "Policies, directives, specifications, standard drawings, or similar documents that are issued by an agency and that change or modify Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions in this Manual shall be considered as supplements to the MUTCD and shall also be revised to be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD." - "After the effective date of a new edition of the MUTCD or a revision thereto, or after the adoption thereof by the State, whichever occurs later, new or reconstructed devices installed shall comply with the new edition or revision, as required by 23 CFR 655.603." Citations: - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.02 Standard §3 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Standard §3 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Support §6 (p.5)

**Gold answer:** They must revise their MUTCDs or supplements into substantial conformance within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes.

## T009

**Question:** When a non-compliant traffic control device is replaced because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, what is the general rule and the limited replacement-in-kind exception?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.03, Section 6K.01, Section 5A.04, Section 1D.10, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or th… | The replacement must generally be a compliant device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The general rule is that a non-compliant device being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable must be replaced with a compliant device. The limited exception permits replacement in kind, based on engineering judgment, when a single compliant device within a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse road users and/or the replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely MUTCD compliance. Standards (mandatory): - "A non-compliant traffic control device that is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) for any reason shall be replaced with a compliant device, except as provided for in Paragraph 13 of this Section." - "Unless a particular device is no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02), non-compliant devices on existing highways and bikeways shall be brought into compliance with the current edition of the National MUTCD as part of the systematic upgrading of substandard traffic control devices (and installation of new required traffic control devices) required pursuant to the Highway Safety Program, 23 U.S.C. §402(a)." - "Channelizing devices that are no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) shall be replaced." Guidance (recommended): - Agencies should establish maintenance policies that incorporate effective practices to identify and then fix or replace in a timely manner any traffic control device that is reaching the end of its useful life, or that is damaged or otherwise no longer serviceable; they should also apply uniform and consistent devices, plan temporary/emergency traffic control with compliant devices, and remove extraneous devices. - Functional maintenance of traffic control devices should be used to determine if certain devices need to be changed to meet current traffic conditions. Options (permitted): - A non-compliant traffic control device may be replaced in kind when engineering judgment indicates it is more appropriate because: (A) one compliant device in the midst of a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would be confusing to road users, and/or (B) the schedule for replacement of the whole series of non-compliant devices will result in achieving timely compliance with the MUTCD. Citations: - Section 1B.03 Standard §12 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Option §13 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §5 (p.5) - Section 6K.01 Standard §15 (p.815) - Section 5A.04 Guidance §6 (p.762) - Section 1D.10 Guidance §1 (p.38)

**Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditio… | The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The experimenting agency must submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment; if no final report (or notice of mitigating circumstances) is received within 6 months of the end of the experimentation period, the experiment is considered terminated and FHWA's approval is rescinded. Similarly, failing to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring the site(s) to be restored to MUTCD compliance within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A new traffic control device is not eligible for official experimentation or interim approval unless it is in the public domain, meaning all proprietary protections (patents, trademarks, copyrights) have been expressly abandoned. Experimentation requests must include a legally-binding certification from the experimenting agency, the manufacturer/developer, and the supplier that the device is unprotected and in the public domain, and acknowledging that any future proprietary protection will result in removal from the MUTCD or cancellation of the approval or authorization. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - "A request for experimentation that involves a new traffic control device or a new application of an existing traffic control device shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation." - "The legally-binding statement shall also state that the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device are aware that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other elements, "a legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Traffic control devices and their supports are prohibited from bearing any advertising message or any message not related to traffic control. This prohibition applies equally to changeable message signs (CMS), their supports, and associated equipment. As explanatory support, States are also encouraged to restrict outdoor advertising from resembling traffic control devices. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices or their supports shall not bear any advertising message or any other message that is not related to traffic control." - "CMS shall display only traffic operational, regulatory, warning, and guidance information except as otherwise provided in this Chapter. Advertising or other messages not related to traffic control shall not be displayed on a CMS or on its supports or other equipment." - "Traffic control devices, public announcements or notices, and other signs or messages within the highway right-of-way shall be placed only as authorized by a public authority or the official having jurisdiction, or, in the case of site roadways or private toll roads open to public travel, by the private owner or private official having jurisdiction, for the purpose of regulating, warning, or guiding traffic." Citations: - Section 1D.07 Standard §1 (p.34) - Section 2L.02 Standard §1 (p.511) - Section 1D.02 Standard §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.02 Support §6 (p.32)

**Gold answer:** No. They may not bear advertising or any message unrelated to traffic control. Acknowledgment signs, Specific Service signs, and Tourist-Oriented Directional signs are not treated as advertising under this provision.

## T016

**Question:** How does the MUTCD classify regulatory, warning, and guide signs by function?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2D.39, Section 2A.03, Section 2F.12, Section 2G.10, Section 2A.13, Section 2B.51. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. | Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. | Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs by function into three principal categories: regulatory signs (which give notice of traffic laws or regulations), warning signs (which give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent), and guide signs (which show routes, destinations, directions, distances, services, and points of interest). This functional classification is a mandatory (Standard) definition, and it carries through to other provisions—for example, when regulatory or warning messages are incorporated into guide signs, they must retain their distinctive color coding. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." Guidance (recommended): - Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except where one sign supplements another (among other listed exceptions), which reflects how signs of different functional classes are typically installed and combined in the field. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates typical installation heights and lateral offsets for signs of different functional classes, including a regulatory STOP sign, a Speed Limit sign, warning signs with advisory speed plaques, route assemblies, and freeway guide signs. - [Image 2] Figure 2A-3 shows placement examples for regulatory STOP and YIELD signs at various intersection configurations. - [Image 3] Figure 2A-4 relates the sign classification framework to the chapters (2B, 2C, and following) that govern each functional class. - [Image 4] Table 2C-3 provides advance placement guidelines specific to warning signs, reflecting their function of alerting drivers to conditions that might not be readily apparent. - [Image 5] This page shows the Chapter 2C table of contents covering warning signs and object markers, illustrating how the warning sign class is organized (horizontal alignment, vertical grade, and roadway geometry warning signs). - [Image 6] This page shows the Chapter 6 table of contents distinguishing TTC zone regulatory signs (Chapter 6G) from TTC zone warning signs (Chapter 6H), reflecting the same functional classification in temporary traffic control. - [Image 7] Table 2A-2 shows common uses of sign colors by functional type, with regulatory signs using black/red/white, warning signs using black on yellow, and guide signs using white on green. - [Image 8] Figure 2B-23 shows an example roundabout application combining regulatory signs (YIELD, ONE WAY, lane-use signs) and warning signs (pedestrian, roundabout circulation), with guide signs cross-referenced to Section 2D.39. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2B.51 Support §6 (p.123) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Figure 2A-4 (p.50) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 23 - Page 9 - Page 85 - Page 162

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

## T017

**Question:** What casing and orientation rules generally apply to word messages on signs?

**Grade:** C=0.60, Comp=0.50, F=0.70, Cit=0.50, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 57.5% (Limited credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

**Rationale:** The response received 57.5% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response contains part of the correct conclusion, but it also omits or misstates a material requirement. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The faithfulness deduction follows the adjudicated issue described below. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 2A.04, Section 1A.05, Section 2D.04, Section 2A.09, Section 2A.08, Section 2D.55. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. | Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters Adjudication note: Correct left-to-right orientation but substitutes a community-wayfinding casing rule for the general sign-message casing rule. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In general, word messages on signs must be aligned horizontally and read left to right, and must use the standard wording shown in the MUTCD and the "Standard Highway Signs" publication. Casing rules depend on the sign type: on community wayfinding guide signs, destination names use lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters, while all other word messages on those signs must be in all upper-case letters. Minor layout modifications to word messages are permitted within standardized designs, provided shapes and colors remain as indicated. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05)." - "The lettering for destinations on community wayfinding guide signs shall be a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (see Section 2D.04). All other word messages on community wayfinding guide signs shall be in all upper-case letters." - "Standardization of these designs does not preclude further improvement by minor modifications to the orientation of symbols (see Section 2A.09), width of borders, or layout of word messages, but all shapes and colors shall be as indicated." Guidance (recommended): - For pavement marking word messages (a related context): "If a pavement marking word message consists of more than one line of information, it should read in the direction of travel. The first word of the message should be nearest to the road user." - On community wayfinding signs, "Word messages should be as brief as practical and the lettering should be large enough to provide the necessary legibility distance." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows CMS message construction examples in which all word messages are displayed in upper-case letters, consistent with the note that single-color CMS examples use all upper-case lettering. [Image 2] illustrates Destination Guide signing on shared-use paths, where destination lettering combines lower-case with initial upper-case letters while other word messages are all upper-case, paralleling the community wayfinding casing rule. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.55 Standard §32 (p.271) - Section 2A.04 Standard §8 (p.40) - Section 3B.21 Guidance §2 (p.575) - Section 2D.55 Guidance §28 (p.271) - Table 2L-3 (p.517) - Figure 9D-5 (p.1082)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A NEW plaque used to call attention to a new sign should stay in place for a period determined by engineering judgment, but no more than 12 months, after which it should be removed. Strobe lights are prohibited as a means of enhancing sign conspicuity. Standards (mandatory): - "Strobe lights shall not be used to enhance the conspicuity of highway signs." Guidance (recommended): - If a NEW plaque is used, it should remain in place for a period of time determined by engineering judgment, but not more than 12 months. - The NEW plaque should be removed no later than 12 months after it was installed. Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, a NEW plaque may be added above a new standard regulatory or warning sign, for a period of time in accordance with Paragraph 3 of Section 2A.11, to call attention to the new sign (one of several permitted conspicuity-enhancement methods, which also include increased sign size, dual signing, and header panels). - A NEW (W16-15P) plaque may be mounted above a regulatory sign when a new regulation takes effect to alert road users, or above an advance warning sign (such as a Signal Ahead sign for a newly-installed traffic control signal) to warn of a new traffic condition. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §4 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Guidance §3 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Guidance §2 (p.197) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Option §1 (p.197)

**Gold answer:** The NEW plaque should remain only for a period determined by engineering judgment and no more than 12 months. Strobe lights may not be used to enhance highway-sign conspicuity.

The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 6F.02, Section 8B.04, Figure 2A-2, Figure 6F-1, Figure 2M-3. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.850 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In rural areas, roadside signs must be mounted at least 5 feet from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (or where the view of the sign might be obstructed), the minimum height is 7 feet, measured to the top of the curb or, absent a curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot/7-foot rule applies to temporary traffic control signs and to Crossbuck Assembly YIELD/STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)," and in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height "shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For grade crossings: the mounting height of a YIELD or STOP sign in a Crossbuck Assembly on a new or separate support "shall be at least 5 feet in rural areas and shall be at least 7 feet in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." Guidance (recommended): - Except as otherwise provided, signs mounted on portable sign supports that do not meet the minimum mounting heights provided in Part 2 should not be used for a duration of more than 3 days. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates a rural roadside STOP sign with "5 ft MIN." height and a business/commercial/residential speed limit sign with "7 ft* MIN." height (*where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur). - [Image 2] Page 831 shows Figure 6F-1 with temporary traffic control signs at 5 ft minimum in rural areas (panels A and B) and 7 ft minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas with and without curb (panels C and D). - [Image 3] Page 92 (the Figure 2A-2 page) depicts typical installations confirming the 5-ft minimum for rural roadside signs and 7-ft minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas. - [Image 4] Page 99 contains the Section 2A.15 Standard text stating the 5-foot rural minimum and the 7-foot minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely. - [Image 5] Page 566 (Figure 2M-3) shows recreational-area sign assemblies mounted at 5 ft minimum in rural areas and 7 ft minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas, consistent with the same height rules. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 8B.04 Standard §12 (p.1000) - Section 6F.02 Guidance §11 (p.791) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 831 - Page 92 - Page 99 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2G.03, Section 2G.08, Section 2G.10, Figure 2A-2, p.51. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a minimum vertical clearance of 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders. A lesser clearance is allowed only where the mounting structure or nearby structures along the roadway have a lesser vertical clearance, and in special cases such as tunnels or double-deck bridges. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." (Section 2A.15, shown in [Image 2]) - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." (Section 2A.15, shown in [Image 2]) Options (permitted): - If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures in order to improve visibility of the overhead signs (shown in [Image 2]). - Preferential Lane regulatory signs post-mounted on a median barrier that are 72 inches or less in width may be mounted higher, such that the vertical clearance to the bottom of the sign, light fixture, or structural support, whichever is lowest, is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders. - Warning signs applicable only to a preferential lane, post-mounted on a median barrier and 72 inches or less in width, may similarly be mounted higher with a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders. - Preferential Lane guide signs 72 inches or less in width may likewise be mounted higher with not less than 17 feet of vertical clearance above any portion of the pavement and shoulders. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, panel H ("Overhead sign"), illustrates the "17 ft MIN." vertical clearance from the pavement to the bottom of an overhead sign, along with the 6-foot lateral offset from the shoulder to the support. - [Image 2] This page contains the Section 2A.15 Standard requiring not less than 17 feet of vertical clearance for overhead signs over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, plus the related exceptions. Citations: - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 100 - Section 2G.03 Option §14 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Option §4 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Option §18 (p.422)

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Regulatory signs must be installed at or near the location where the regulations they convey actually apply, and they must clearly indicate the requirements and be designed and installed for adequate visibility and legibility. For nighttime visibility, every regulatory sign is required to be either retroreflective or illuminated, per Section 2A.21. Standards (mandatory): - "Regulatory signs shall be used to inform road users of selected traffic laws or regulations and to indicate the applicability of the legal requirements." - "Regulatory signs shall be installed at or near where the regulations apply. The signs shall clearly indicate the requirements imposed by the regulations and shall be designed and installed to provide adequate visibility and legibility in order to obtain compliance." - "Regulatory signs shall be retroreflective or illuminated (see Section 2A.21)." - For preferential lanes: "If used, overhead Preferential Lane (R3-13 series, R3-14 series, and R3-15 series) regulatory signs shall be installed on the side of the roadway where the entrance to the preferential lane is located and any appropriate adjustments shall be made to the sign message." Guidance (recommended): - At toll plazas, regulatory signs (or regulatory panels within guide signs) indicating vehicle-type restrictions and accepted forms of toll payment should be installed over the applicable lane, either on the toll plaza canopy or on a separate structure immediately in advance of it, so each sign is clearly related to an individual toll lane. Options (permitted): - Where ramp control signals meter traffic on a freeway or expressway entrance ramp, regulatory signs with legends appropriate to the control may be installed adjacent to the ramp control signal faces. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2A-4 lists the acceptable means of retroreflection for sign elements (e.g., prismatic reflector "buttons" and smooth-surfaced microstructure materials for the symbol, word message, and border), supporting the retroreflectivity requirement referenced by Section 2A.21. [Image 2] Page TC-2 (table of contents) shows the location of Sections 2A.21 (Retroreflection and Illumination) and Chapter 2B (Regulatory Signs). [Image 3] Page 996 illustrates regulatory signs and plaques for grade crossings, examples of regulatory signs subject to these placement and visibility requirements. [Image 5] Page 1053 illustrates regulatory signs and plaques for bicycle facilities, further examples of regulatory signs covered by these requirements. **Gold answer:** They must be installed at or near the location where the regulation applies, clearly state the requirement, and provide adequate visibility and legibility. They must be retroreflective or illuminated.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At an all-way stop intersection, an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque is required to be mounted below each STOP sign, with a white legend and border on a red background. Plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY (or other numbers of ways) are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." Guidance (recommended): - The TRAFFIC FROM LEFT (RIGHT) DOES NOT STOP (W4-4aP) plaque or ONCOMING TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4bP) plaque should be used at intersections where STOP signs control all but one approach, unless the only non-stopped approach is from a one-way street. Options (permitted): - The CROSS TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4P) plaque may be used with a STOP sign when engineering judgment indicates conditions could cause road users to misinterpret the intersection as an all-way stop. - The W4-4aP or W4-4bP plaque may be used when such messages more accurately describe the traffic controls established at the intersection. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque with white legend on a red background alongside the STOP (R1-1) sign and related plaques. [Image 2] shows page 74 containing Section 2B.04's Standard provisions requiring the R1-3P plaque and prohibiting numbered-way plaques, together with Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §5 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Guidance §7 (p.74) - Section 2C.66 Guidance §3 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §1 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §2 (p.198) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A YIELD sign is mandatory at the entrance to a roundabout to require road users to yield to other traffic, and YIELD signs must be placed to control the approach roadways only. They shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway itself. Standards (mandatory): - "A YIELD sign shall be used to require road users to yield the right-of-way to other traffic at the entrance to a roundabout. YIELD signs at roundabouts shall be used to control the approach roadways and shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway." Guidance (recommended): - On an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign, and at uncontrolled exits, crosswalks should be located a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Options (permitted): - YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts. - At roundabouts where ONE WAY signs have been installed in the central island, Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques may be placed below the YIELD signs on approaches to supplement the central island signs. - The Vehicular Traffic Warning sign assembly may be installed on an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. - The Non-Vehicular Warning sign assembly may likewise be installed on such a YIELD-controlled approach where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows YIELD word markings, yield lines, and dotted edge-line extensions on the entering approach roadways only — with the "20 ft MIN." crosswalk setback dimensioned — and no yield control on the circulatory roadway. - [Image 2] Figure 3D-4 shows a two-lane roundabout where yield markings and dotted entrance lines appear only at the approach entries, while the circulatory roadway carries lane-use arrows and lane lines rather than any yield control. - [Image 3] This full-page view contains Sections 3D.04–3D.05, confirming that yield lines and YIELD/YIELD AHEAD word markings are optional treatments on roundabout approaches. - [Image 4] This full-page view contains Section 2B.51, showing the option to place Roundabout Circulation plaques below YIELD signs on roundabout approaches. - [Image 5] This full-page view contains Section 2B.10, including the Standard that YIELD signs at roundabouts shall control the approach roadways and shall not control the circulatory roadway. - [Image 6] This full-page view contains Section 3C.09, including the guidance that crosswalks on YIELD-controlled approaches be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Citations: - Section 2B.10 Standard §5 (p.78) - Section 3C.09 Guidance §3 (p.593) - Section 3D.05 Option §1 (p.605) - Section 2B.51 Option §4 (p.118) - Section 2C.54 Option §11 (p.192) - Section 2C.55 Option §6 (p.193) - Figure 3D-1 (p.596) - Figure 3D-4 (p.600) - Page 646 - Page 159 - Page 119 - Page 634

**Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The provided evidence does not contain the actual hourly volume thresholds of All-Way Stop Control Warrant D — those values appear in Items A and B of Paragraph 1 of Section 2B.16, which is not included in the evidence supplied. What the evidence does show is the speed-based adjustment: when the 85th-percentile approach speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the Item A and Item B values. (The other provisions supplied — Sections 4C.02, 4C.03, 4C.05, and 4C.08 — concern traffic signal warrants, not All-Way Stop Control Warrant D.) Options (permitted): - "If the 85th-percentile approach speed of the major-street traffic exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the values given in Items A and B in Paragraph 1 of this Section." If you can supply Paragraph 1 of Section 2B.16, I can complete the answer.

**Gold answer:** For the same 8 hours of a typical day, the combined entering volume must be at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor-street approaches. If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A Speed Limit (R2-1) sign must display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study, and the displayed speed limits must be in multiples of 5 mph. A Speed Limit sign should not be used to show an advisory speed for a roadway condition; instead, an Advisory Speed plaque mounted below a warning sign is the recommended (and permitted) device for that purpose. Standards (mandatory): - "The Speed Limit (R2-1) sign (see Figure 2B-3) shall display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study. The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - For variable speed limit signs: "The regulatory speed displayed on a variable Speed Limit sign shall comply with Paragraph 13 of" Section 2B.21 (i.e., the same display requirements apply). Guidance (recommended): - As shown in the page text visible in [Image 3]: "An advisory speed plaque (see Section 2C.59) mounted below a warning sign should be used to warn road users of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. A Speed Limit sign should not be used for this purpose." Options (permitted): - "An Advisory Speed plaque (see Figure 2C-1 and Section 2C.59) may be used when the change in roadway condition requires a reduced speed." - "An Advisory Exit Speed or Advisory Ramp Speed sign may be used based on engineering judgment when the difference between the mainline roadway speed limit and the exit or ramp advisory speed in the vicinity of the departure is 10 mph or less." - "A Speed Limit Sign Beacon may be used with a fixed or variable Speed Limit sign," and a flashing beacon with an appropriate accompanying sign may indicate that the displayed speed limit is in effect. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-3 shows the Speed Limit (R2-1) sign displaying "SPEED LIMIT 50" along with related speed limit signs and plaques (e.g., R2-2P TRUCKS, R2-4P MINIMUM, R2-4a SPEED LIMIT/MINIMUM), all with limits in multiples of 5 mph. - [Image 2] Shows curve warning sign layouts where advisory speeds (35 MPH) are conveyed by W13-1P Advisory Speed plaques mounted below warning signs (W1-2, W1-8), not by Speed Limit signs. - [Image 3] Shows the text of Section 2B.21, including Standard Paragraph 13 (display of the established limit in multiples of 5 mph) and the Guidance that a Speed Limit sign should not be used to warn of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. - [Image 4] Shows Figure 2B-3 (Speed Limit Signs and Plaques) with the R2-1 sign and related plaques, all displaying speeds in multiples of 5 mph. - [Image 5] Shows examples of enhanced conspicuity for signs, including a SPEED LIMIT 35 sign with flags, illustrating regulatory (not advisory) use of the R2-1 sign. Citations: - Section 2B.21 Standard §13 (p.85) - Section 2G.26 Standard §3 (p.457) - Section 2C.28 Option §2 (p.173) - Section 2C.12 Option §5 (p.160) - Section 4S.04 Option §3 (p.753) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86) - Page 126

**Gold answer:** It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. It should not be used for an advisory speed; an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead.

The DO NOT ENTER sign is expressly mandated at the intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad, and Figure 2B-15 illustrates the coordinated placement of all three sign types (along with STOP or YIELD control at the ramp terminal). (This is a related countermeasure, not one of the three sign types.) Options (permitted): - A Directional assembly with a diagonal downward-pointing arrow (M6-2aP) plaque may be used at the far left-hand corner of an intersection with a freeway or expressway entrance ramp to "distinguish the entrance ramp from an adjoining exit ramp terminal at the same intersection with the conventional road," helping road users avoid entering the wrong ramp. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-15 depicts the exit-ramp terminal treatment: paired DO NOT ENTER signs facing the crossroad, back-to-back ONE WAY sign assemblies on both sides of the ramp, and WRONG WAY signs facing potential wrong-way traffic down the exit ramp, plus STOP or YIELD control at the ramp terminal and wrong-way pavement arrows. - [Image 2] This full-page view of Figure 2B-15 ("Example of Application of Regulatory Signing and Pavement Markings at an Exit Ramp Termination to Deter Wrong-Way Entry," Sect. 2B.48) confirms the same three sign types arranged around the exit-ramp/crossroad intersection. - [Image 3] This page shows Section 2B.46, whose Standard mandates the DO NOT ENTER sign at "the intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad as specified in Section 2B.48 (see Figure 2B-15)," and Figure 2B-13 displaying the R5-1 (DO NOT ENTER), R5-1a (WRONG WAY), and R6-1/R6-2 (ONE WAY) signs. - [Image 4] The table of contents lists Figure 2B-15 ("Example of Application of Regulatory Signing and Pavement Markings at an Exit Ramp Termination to Deter Wrong-Way Entry") and related figures 2B-13 and 2B-17 pairing DO NOT ENTER, WRONG WAY, and ONE WAY signing. - [Image 5] Figure 2D-18's notes direct the reader to "See Section 2B.48 and Figure 2B-15 for an example of regulatory signing and pavement markings at an exit ramp termination to deter wrong-way entry," confirming Section 2B.48 as the governing provision. Citations: - Figure 2B-15 (p.112) - Page 153 - Page 150 - Page 29 - Page 297 - Section 2D.45 Guidance §3 (p.247) - Section 2D.50 Option §3 (p.254)

**Gold answer:** At least one ONE WAY sign for each crossroad travel direction, at least one DO NOT ENTER sign near the downstream end of the exit ramp, and at least one WRONG WAY sign on the exit ramp facing wrong-way road users are required.

The package retains the official deduction without inventing a new source citation. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6B.04, Section 6H.02, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1, Figure 2C-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.501 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs is a Guidance (recommended) matter based on providing adequate Perception-Response Time. For permanent warning signs, Table 2C-3 gives recommended advance placement distances keyed to the posted or 85th-percentile speed and the condition being warned of (Condition A: speed reduction/lane changing in heavy traffic; Condition B: deceleration to a listed advisory speed). For temporary traffic control (TTC) zones, signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at distances that vary with roadway type, condition, and posted speed (e.g., 4–8 times the speed limit on urban streets, 8–12 times on rural highways, and ½ mile or more on freeways and expressways). Guidance (recommended): - Warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed; where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign should be placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways (see Table 6B-1). - On freeways and expressways, advance warning sign placement should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow, extending as far as ½ mile or more. - On urban streets, the effective placement of the nearest warning sign to the TTC zone, in feet, should range from 4 to 8 times the speed limit in mph, with the high end of the range used when speeds are relatively high; when two or more advance warning signs are used on higher-speed streets, the advance warning area should extend a greater distance. - On rural highways, the effective placement of the first warning sign should be substantially longer—8 to 12 times the speed limit in mph—and the advance warning area should extend 1,500 feet or more for open highway conditions. - The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions; Table 2C-3 provides guideline distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A (speed reduction and lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to a listed advisory speed). - Per Table 2C-3's notes, for warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] shows Table 2C-3, "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs," listing recommended distances (e.g., 225 ft at 20 mph up to 1,600 ft at 85 mph for Condition A) with footnotes explaining legibility-distance adjustments and PRT/deceleration assumptions. - [Image 2] shows Section 2C.04, Placement of Warning Signs, with the Guidance that signs should provide adequate PRT and that Table 2C-3 distances should be applied with engineering judgment, along with the full Table 2C-3. - [Image 3] shows Sections 6H.01–6H.02, including the Guidance that TTC advance warning signs should be placed at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed, per Table 6B-1. - [Image 4] shows Figure 2C-2 (horizontal alignment curve example), whose notes direct users to Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines. - [Image 5] shows Section 6B.04, Advance Warning Area, with the Guidance on 4–8 times the speed limit for urban streets, 8–12 times for rural highways, and ½ mile or more for freeways and expressways. Citations: - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §4 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §6 (p.771) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Table 2C-3 (p.152)

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.
