Their use to emphasize a change in horizontal alignment must also conform to Table 2C-4, and their spacing along the curve should follow Table 2C-5. Standards (mandatory): - "The Chevron Alignment sign shall be a vertical rectangle. No border shall be used on the Chevron Alignment sign." - "If used, Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed on the outside of a turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed at a minimum height of 4 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way." - "The use of the Chevron Alignment (W1-8) sign (see Figures 2C-1 and 2C-2) to provide additional emphasis and guidance for a change in horizontal alignment shall be in accordance with the information shown in Table 2C-4." Guidance (recommended): - The Chevron Alignment signs should be visible for a sufficient distance to provide the road user with adequate time to react to the change in alignment. - When the Hairpin Curve sign or the 270-degree Loop sign is installed, either a One-Direction Large Arrow (W1-6) sign or Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs should be installed on the outside of the turn or curve. Options (permitted): - Chevron Alignment signs may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2C-5, which gives the typical spacing of Chevron Alignment signs on horizontal curves by advisory speed and curve radius (e.g., 40 feet for 15 mph or less, up to 200 feet for more than 60 mph). The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the MUTCD's treatment of changeable message signs, a unit of information is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision (e.g., "What happened?" → "MAJOR CRASH"). As Guidance (recommended practice), a unit of information should not be more than four words, and the total number of units in a message governs its maximum length. Guidance (recommended): - "A unit of information, which is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision, should not be more than four words." The maximum message length should be dictated by the number of units of information plus the size of the CMS. - Related principles: no more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase; no more than four units in a message when traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more; no more than five units when speeds are less than 35 mph; and only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS. Options (permitted): - A unit of information consisting of more than one word may be displayed on more than one line, and an additional downstream CMS may be used so the entire message can be read twice. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-2 illustrates four units of information as question/answer pairs (What happened? → MAJOR CRASH; Where? → AT EXIT 12; Who is the advisory for? → drivers heading TO NEW YORK; What is advised? → USE ROUTE 46), and shows how they can be arranged into a two-phase message ("MAJOR CRASH / AT EXIT 12" and "USE ROUTE 46 / TO NEW YORK"). Citations: - Section 2L.05 Guidance §1 (p.514) - Section 2L.05 Guidance §7 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Option §9 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §2 (p.515) - Section 2L.05 Support §3 (p.515) - Table 2L-2 (p.515)

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

## T031

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 9E.13, Figure 3B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.951 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yellow center line markings communicate that the traffic lanes on either side of the marking carry traffic in opposite directions of travel, while white lane line markings communicate that the lanes on either side carry traffic in the same direction of travel. In short, color conveys directionality: yellow separates opposing flows and white separates same-direction flows. The specific pattern of the yellow line (broken vs. solid) further communicates whether passing across the center line is permitted or prohibited. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - On two-lane, two-way roadways, the center line markings shall be one of the following as shown in Figure 3B-1: "A. Two-direction passing zone markings consisting of a normal width broken yellow line where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for traffic traveling in either direction; B. One-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of a double yellow line, one of which is a normal width broken yellow line and the other is a normal width solid yellow line, where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for the traffic traveling adjacent to the broken line, but is prohibited for traffic traveling adjacent to the solid line; or C. Two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of two normal width solid yellow lines where crossing the center line markings for passing is prohibited for traffic traveling in either direction." - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." Options (permitted): - Solid white lane line markings may be used: on approaches to intersections to separate contiguous through lanes; to separate through traffic lanes from auxiliary lanes, such as an added uphill truck lane; and on approaches to crosswalks across multi-lane roadways. - On shared-use paths wide enough for two minimum-width lanes, a solid yellow center line may be used to separate the two directions of travel where passing or traveling to the left of the line is not permitted, and a broken yellow center line may be used where passing is permitted. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3B-2 shows a multi-lane, two-way roadway where double solid yellow lines separate the opposing directions (arrows pointing opposite ways) while broken white lane lines separate same-direction lanes. - [Image 2] Figure 3B-3 shows three-lane, two-way applications where the yellow center line (broken/solid combinations) marks the boundary between opposing directions and white lane lines separate the two same-direction lanes. - [Image 3] Figure 3B-1 illustrates two-lane, two-way yellow center line patterns—broken, broken-plus-solid, and double solid—corresponding to passing zones, one-direction no-passing zones, and two-direction no-passing zones. - [Image 4] The full-page view of Figure 3B-2 (p.541) confirms the double yellow center line dividing opposing arrows with white lane lines between same-direction lanes, including single-lane left-turn channelization delineated by yellow lines. - [Image 5] The full-page view of Figure 3B-3 (p.542) shows both passing-permitted and passing-prohibited three-lane configurations, with yellow marking the directional divide. - [Image 6] Page 595 (Figure 3B-11) shows white lane-drop and channelizing lines separating same-direction freeway lanes, with the yellow line only at the left edge of the directional roadway. - [Image 7] Page 674 (Figure 3I-1) shows two-way streets where tubular markers supplement the yellow center line separating opposing flows and white lane lines separating same-direction lanes. Citations: - Section 3B.01 Standard §1 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Standard §1 (p.546) - Section 3B.01 Standard §4 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §6 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Option §8 (p.548) - Section 9E.13 Option §1 (p.1106) - Figure 3B-1 (p.540) - Figure 3B-2 (p.541) - Figure 3B-3 (p.542) - Page 582 - Page 583 - Page 595 - Page 674

**Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.

## T032

**Question:** When are no-passing-zone markings required at horizontal or vertical curves, and what should be done when successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.12, Section 3B.13, Section 8C.02, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.03, Section 2B.36. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones. | They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On roadways with center line markings, no-passing-zone markings are mandatory at horizontal or vertical curves wherever the passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit. More generally, on two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways with center lines, no-passing zones must be established at curves and other locations where an engineering study shows passing must be prohibited. When successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, it is recommended that the markings connect the zones. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Crosswalk markings must be white, and transverse lines, when used, must be between 6 inches and 24 inches wide. The marked crosswalk itself must be at least 6 feet wide, increasing to a minimum of 8 feet at non-intersection crosswalks where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater. The same 6-to-24-inch limit applies to the transverse lines that establish the limits of a ladder crosswalk. Standards (mandatory): - "The transverse lines used to establish the limits of the ladder crosswalk shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." - As shown in the page evidence for Section 3C.03: "Crosswalk markings shall be white. When used, transverse lines shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." [Image 2] - Also shown in Section 3C.03: "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, the minimum width of a marked crosswalk shall be 6 feet," and "At a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater, the minimum width of the crosswalk shall be 8 feet." [Image 2] Guidance (recommended): - Transverse line crosswalk markings should be limited to locations controlled by traffic control signals or on approaches controlled by STOP or YIELD signs. - To provide contrast with aesthetic surface treatments, a gap of at least one-half the width of the white transverse line used to establish the crosswalk, but not less than 6 inches, should be used between the white crosswalk lines and the aesthetic surface treatment, such as unmarked pavement or a black contrast line. Options (permitted): - High-visibility crosswalk markings may be used where additional conspicuity is desired for a crosswalk over transverse line crosswalk markings. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3C-1 depicts the dimensional limits for each crosswalk design: transverse lines 6–24 inches wide with a 6 ft minimum crosswalk width; longitudinal bars 12–60 inches long (not to exceed 2.5 times the bar width) and 12–24 inches wide; ladder designs combining both; and bar pairs of 8–12-inch bars, all with a footnote that the minimum crosswalk width shall be 8 feet where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater at a non-intersection crosswalk. - [Image 2] Page 631 shows Section 3C.03 (Design of Crosswalk Markings), including the Standard that crosswalk markings shall be white, transverse lines shall be 6–24 inches wide, and the 6-foot (or 8-foot) minimum crosswalk widths, together with Figure 3C-1. - [Image 3] Page 632 shows Sections 3C.04 (Transverse Line Crosswalks) and 3C.05 (High-Visibility Crosswalks), including the guidance limiting transverse line crosswalks to signal- or STOP/YIELD-controlled locations. - [Image 4] Page 17 is a table of contents listing Chapter 3C Crosswalk Markings sections, confirming section locations but adding no dimensional requirements. - [Image 5] Page 664 shows Section 3H.03/3H.04 material and Figure 3H-1, including the guidance on a contrast gap of at least half the transverse line width (not less than 6 inches) between crosswalk lines and aesthetic surface treatments. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Section 3C.04 Guidance §1 (p.591) - Section 3H.03 Guidance §11 (p.623) - Section 3C.05 Option §1 (p.591) - Section 3C.01 Support §1 (p.589) - Section 3C.01 Support §2 (p.589) - Figure 3C-1 (p.590) - Page 631 - Page 632 - Page 664

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.85, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 96.2% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 96.2% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. | A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4C.01, Section 2B.12, Section 4B.03, Section 4C.08, p.651, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. | A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that meeting a signal warrant does not by itself require installing a traffic control signal. Warrant satisfaction is only one factor in an engineering study, and alternatives to signals should be considered even when warrants are met. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - Similarly, for the parallel case of stop control: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Guidance (recommended): - Because road user delay and the frequency of some types of crashes are sometimes higher under signal control than under STOP sign control, consideration should be given to providing alternatives to traffic control signals even if one or more signal warrants has been satisfied. - Where volumes must be estimated (e.g., locations under development or construction), a signal installed under projected conditions should have an engineering study done within 1 year of steady (stop-and-go) operation to determine if it is justified; if not justified, the signal should be taken out of steady operation or removed (except where Warrant 8 was used to justify the signal). Support (explanatory): - The safe and efficient movement of all road users is the primary consideration in the engineering study; warrant satisfaction is one relevant factor but is not intended to be the only factor or overriding consideration. Signal installation at an inappropriate location could adversely impact safety, and agencies can even install a signal where no warrants are met if an engineering study documents the rationale. - The Crash Experience warrant illustrates that individual warrants address specific reasons (e.g., crash severity and frequency) for considering a signal, not a mandate to install one. Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79) - Section 4B.03 Guidance §1 (p.649) - Section 4C.01 Guidance §12 (p.652) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Section 4C.08 Support §1 (p.661)

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, Section 4F.01, Section 4F.02, Section 4F.09, p.644, p.682. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN indication is generally permitted to proceed straight through, turn right or left, or make a U-turn, unless those movements are modified by other traffic control devices such as lane-use signs, turn prohibitions, markings, or separate turn signals. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk and to other vehicles lawfully within the intersection; drivers turning left or making a left U-turn must additionally yield to oncoming vehicles that are close enough to be an immediate hazard. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." Options (permitted): - A steady straight-through GREEN ARROW signal indication may be used instead of a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication in a signal face to discourage wrong-way turns under specified conditions (e.g., approaches intersecting a one-way street or an interchange exit ramp, unique geometric design prohibiting turns, or approaches with pre-signals where adjacent lanes are controlled separately). Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644) - Section 4F.01 Option §5 (p.682) - Section 4F.02 Support §2 (p.683) - Section 4F.09 Support §2 (p.693)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The yellow change interval's exclusive function is to warn approaching traffic that their permission to proceed is being terminated, after which they will be directed to stop (or must complete a turn permissively in the case of a protected/permissive movement). Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, and for emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons it should last between 3 and 6 seconds. The provided evidence contains no provision addressing pre-yellow vehicular countdown displays, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." Guidance (recommended): - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons, a yellow change interval should have a minimum duration of 3 seconds and a maximum duration of 6 seconds, with the longer intervals reserved for approaches with higher speeds. Citations: - Section 4F.17 Support §1 (p.708) - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4H.11 Support §3 (p.717) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4N.03 Guidance §5 (p.743)

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.

## T038

**Question:** What walking speed is generally used to calculate pedestrian clearance time, and what alternative speed may be used with an extended push-button feature?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Section 4K.05, p.724, p.725, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. | Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The pedestrian clearance time is generally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, measured from the curb or edge of pavement to the far side of the traveled way or a sufficient median. Where an extended push button press function is installed to let slower pedestrians request more crossing time, a walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used instead. Additionally, the combined walk interval and clearance time should accommodate a slower 3 feet per second speed measured from the pedestrian detector, and slower speeds should be considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Guidance (recommended): - Except as provided in Paragraph 8, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication "to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the pedestrian detector (or, if no detector is present, a location 6 feet behind the face of the curb or edge of pavement) at the beginning of the WALKING PERSON indication to travel at a walking speed of 3 feet per second to the far side of the traveled way (or median for a two-stage crossing); any additional time required should be added to the walk interval. Options (permitted): - "A walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the pedestrian clearance time at locations where an extended push button press function has been installed to provide slower pedestrians an opportunity to request and receive a longer pedestrian clearance time." Passive pedestrian detection may also be used to automatically adjust the clearance time based on the pedestrian's actual walking speed or actual clearance of the crosswalk. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - Pedestrians may be provided with additional features such as increased crossing time, audible beaconing, or a speech push button information message as a result of an extended push button press. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §8 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §14 (p.725) - Section 4K.05 Option §1 (p.734)

**Gold answer:** The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time.

## T039

**Question:** When is a countdown display mandatory on a pedestrian signal head?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.04, Section 9E.12, p.720, p.1105. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory on all pedestrian signal heads at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval exceeds 7 seconds. It is also mandatory when a bicycle box extends across more than one motor-vehicle approach lane, in which case the countdown must display without actuation. Where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less, a countdown display is permitted but not required. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "The countdown pedestrian signal shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication." - "Countdown pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.04) for the crosswalk or pedestrian crossing movement that crosses the approach shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation." Options (permitted): - Pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less may include a pedestrian change interval countdown display. Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 9E.12 Standard §8 (p.1105) - Section 4I.04 Option §2 (p.720)

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4J.03, Section 4J.02, Figure 4J-3, p.730, p.729, p.728. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, a pedestrian hybrid beacon must display a flashing circular yellow, then a steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red indications during the pedestrian walk interval, and then alternating flashing circular red indications during the pedestrian change interval, after which the faces revert to dark. Between actuations, the beacon must remain dark. Optional additions include a steady red clearance interval after the steady yellow and a short flashing-red buffer interval after the change interval. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Guidance (recommended): - If the pedestrian hybrid beacon is coordinated as part of a signal system, it should remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation is received until the point in the background cycle when the predetermined duration of the flashing yellow interval needs to be initiated to achieve the appropriate coordinated offset. - If installed within a signal system, the pedestrian hybrid beacon should be coordinated. Options (permitted): - If a minimum dark time between activations has been set on the controller, the pedestrian hybrid beacon may remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation has been received until the minimum dark time has been provided. - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after the steady yellow interval (Interval 3), and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after the flashing red change interval (Interval 5). Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 illustrates the six-step sequence: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) steady red during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until activated, with a note describing the optional steady red clearance and buffer intervals. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §7 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Guidance §4 (p.729) - Section 4J.03 Option §8 (p.730) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself, with high visual contrast, aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk so pedestrians can identify the correct button and align with the crosswalk. A locator tone is also required to help pedestrians with vision disabilities find the arrow and the push button. The locator tone may be deactivated during the steady DONT WALK phase only if passive pedestrian detection activates it whenever a pedestrian is within a 12-foot radius. Standards (mandatory): - "Each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." Guidance (recommended): - In temporary traffic control contexts, if a pushbutton is used to provide equivalent TTC information to pedestrians with vision disabilities, it should be equipped with a locator tone to notify pedestrians that a special accommodation is available and to help them locate the pushbutton. Options (permitted): - The push button locator tone may default to a deactivated mode during periods when the steady UPRAISED HAND (DONT WALK) indication is displayed, if a passive pedestrian detection system activates the locator tone at all times (other than when the audible walk indication is active) that a pedestrian is present within a 12-foot radius of the push button; where pedestrian facilities such as sidewalks are present, the passive detection requirement may be limited to pedestrians on those facilities within the 12-foot radius. Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 6C.03 Guidance §8 (p.781) - Section 4K.04 Option §5 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Support §3 (p.734) - Section 4K.01 Support §11 (p.731)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs must remain dark until activated by a pedestrian, flash rapidly during a predetermined period (or until the pedestrian clears the crosswalk with passive detection), and then return to dark. All RRFB units serving a crosswalk must start and stop flashing in unison, and the flash rate is required to be 75 flashing sequences per minute—much faster than standard beacons. The duration of operation is recommended to be based on pedestrian clearance timing procedures. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." Guidance (recommended): - The minimum duration of the predetermined period of RRFB operation following each actuation should be based on the procedures for timing pedestrian clearance times for pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.06). Options (permitted): - A pedestrian-activated and/or bicyclist-activated RRFB may be used to provide supplemental emphasis to pedestrian, school, and trail warning signs at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches. - At intersections with two crosswalks on an uncontrolled approach, post-mounted RRFBs may be installed to face only one direction of travel at the first crosswalk that traffic encounters; when activated, the RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease their rapid flashing indications. Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Guidance §3 (p.738) - Section 4L.01 Option §1 (p.736) - Section 4L.02 Option §11 (p.738)

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes that road user and worker safety and accessibility should be an integral and high-priority element of every project, from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers, and TTC planning is intended to provide continuity of movement and accessible passage when a roadway's normal function is suspended. These are Guidance (recommended) provisions rather than mandatory Standards. Guidance (recommended): - Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction; maintenance and utility work should be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers considered at all times, and if the TTC zone includes a grade crossing, early coordination with the railroad company or light rail transit agency should take place. - General plans or guidelines should be developed to provide safety for motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians, workers, enforcement/emergency officials, and equipment; the basic safety principles governing the design of permanent roadways and roadsides should also govern the design of TTC zones, routing road users through such zones with geometrics, roadside features, and TTC devices as nearly as possible comparable to normal highway situations; a TTC plan appropriate to the project's complexity should be prepared and understood by all responsible parties before the site is occupied, with changes approved by a knowledgeable official; and road user movement should be inhibited as little as practical, avoiding frequent and abrupt changes in geometrics. - Temporary traffic control operations should minimize the inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic, with prior notice given to affected public or private agencies, emergency services, businesses, railroad companies or transit agencies, and road users before free movement is infringed upon or blocked. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766) - Section 6A.02 Guidance §2 (p.768) - Section 8A.13 Guidance §7 (p.992) - Section 6A.01 Support §3 (p.765) - Section 8A.13 Support §1 (p.992)

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Guidance (recommended): - For flagger wear during nighttime activity, high-visibility safety apparel meeting the Performance Class 3 requirements of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 (or equivalent revision), labeled as meeting the Class 3 risk exposure performance, should be worn. - All on-scene responders and news media personnel should constantly be aware of their visibility to oncoming traffic and wear high-visibility apparel. Options (permitted): - Emergency and incident responders and law enforcement personnel within the TTC zone may wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting the performance requirements of ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 ("American National Standard for High-Visibility Public Safety Vests"), or equivalent revisions, labeled as ANSI 207-2006, in lieu of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 apparel. Citations: - Section 6C.05 Standard §1 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §3 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §5 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Guidance §7 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §7 (p.854) - Section 6C.05 Option §4 (p.782)

**Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under MUTCD Guidance, a flagger should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques, and should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate five specific abilities: communicating instructions, maneuvering to avoid errant vehicles, controlling signaling devices, applying proper traffic control practices under stress, and recognizing dangerous traffic situations in time to warn workers. Guidance (recommended): - Flaggers should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques because they are responsible for public safety and make the greatest number of contacts with the public of all highway workers. - Flaggers should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate: (A) ability to receive and communicate specific instructions clearly, firmly, and courteously; (B) ability to move and maneuver quickly in order to avoid danger from errant vehicles; (C) ability to control signaling devices (such as paddles and flags) in order to provide clear and positive guidance to drivers approaching a TTC zone in frequently changing situations; (D) ability to understand and apply proper traffic control practices, sometimes in stressful or emergency situations; and (E) ability to recognize dangerous traffic situations and warn workers in sufficient time to avoid injury. - Where manual traffic control is needed, it should be provided by qualified flaggers or uniformed law enforcement officers. Citations: - Section 6D.01 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §16 (p.855)

**Gold answer:** A flagger should communicate instructions clearly and courteously; move quickly to avoid danger; control paddles or flags to give clear guidance; understand and apply traffic-control practices under stress; and recognize dangerous situations and warn workers in time.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6H.17, Section 6E.01, Section 6N.14, Section 6E.02, Section 6E.05, Section 6N.09. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.947 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When two-way traffic must use a single lane for a limited distance, the movements from each end of the section must be coordinated (with one exception for short, self-regulating situations on low-volume roads). Permitted control methods include flaggers (one or two) and temporary traffic control signals. Where two-way traffic is carried on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing traffic must be physically separated; markings and signing alone are not sufficient. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - "When two-lane, two-way traffic control must be maintained on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing vehicular traffic shall be separated with either temporary traffic barriers (concrete safety-shape or approved alternate), channelizing devices, Narrow Two-Way Traffic (W6-4) signs on flexible supports (see Section 6H.17), or a temporary raised island throughout the length of the two-way operation. The use of markings and complementary signing, by themselves, shall not be used." Options (permitted): - If the zone is short enough for a flagger to see from one end to the other, traffic may be controlled by either a single flagger or by a flagger at each end of the section. - If the work space on a low-volume street or road is short and road users from both directions can see approaching opposing traffic through and beyond the worksite, movement through the one-lane, two-way constriction may be self-regulating. - Traffic control signals may be used to control vehicular traffic movements in one-lane, two-way TTC zones. Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6N.14 Standard §2 (p.850) - Section 6E.02 Option §2 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Option §4 (p.787) - Section 6E.05 Option §1 (p.788) - Section 6N.09 Support §6 (p.846)

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The School Speed Limit assembly (or S5-1 sign) must be placed at, or as near as practicable to, the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be installed in advance of the first School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 sign encountered in each direction. The downstream end of the zone must normally be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, although an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign may be used when the reduced speed limit zone ends at the same point as a designated school zone. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "The School Speed Limit assembly shall be either a static sign assembly, a blank-out sign, or a changeable message sign (see Chapter 2L)." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." Options (permitted): - "The downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign (see Figures 7B-1 and 7B-2)." (Where the reduced school speed limit zone ends at the same point as the designated school zone, the S5-2 sign appears as an alternative to the S5-3 sign in the figures.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the School (S1-1) sign in advance of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the beginning of the zone, with the downstream end marked by an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign, optionally paired with a Speed Limit 45 (R2-1) sign. [Image 2] Figure 7B-4 illustrates the same layout for a school zone with higher fines only for speeding, again showing the S1-1 sign in advance, the School Speed Limit assembly at the zone's start, and S5-3 or S5-2 signs at the downstream end. [Image 3] Figure 7B-1 depicts the components of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 sign, and S4-1P/S4-2P/S4-4P/S4-6P bottom plaques) as well as the END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) and END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) signs. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §6 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Option §7 (p.976) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973)

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box. | The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicyc… Adjudication note: Correct geometry, symbol, and green-pavement rule; omits the turn-on-red prohibition. If green-colored pavement is used, it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box, and bicycle boxes are among the only applications where green-colored pavement is permitted. The evidence provided does not contain further operational restrictions (such as signal or turn restrictions) for bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes..., B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections..., C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane in which motor vehicles must weave across bicyclists in bicycle lanes..., D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes..., E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols..." Guidance (recommended): - If green-colored pavement is used within separated bicycle lanes on an independent alignment, it should be used only at the entrances to those facilities from roadways open to public travel or at conflict, weaving, or crossing locations. Options (permitted): - Green-colored pavement may be installed for the entire length of a bicycle lane or bicycle lane extension or for only a portion (or portions) of it — but note that within a bicycle box specifically, the Standard requires full coverage of the box if green pavement is used. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Guidance §5 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Option §7 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Support §1 (p.625)

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

The evidence is insufficient to cite any exact percentage, because none exists in these provisions. Guidance (recommended): - Minor-road stop control should be considered when, among other conditions, crash records show three or more reported crashes in a 12-month period (or six or more in 36 months for a four-leg intersection; five or more in 36 months for a three-leg intersection), where "The crashes should be susceptible to correction by installation of minor-road stop control" — this is a criterion for consideration, not a guaranteed reduction. Options (permitted): - All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates five or more reported crashes in 12 months (or six or more in 36 months) at a four-leg intersection, or four or more in 12 months (five or more in 36 months) at a three-leg intersection, of a type susceptible to correction by all-way stop control — again a warrant, not a performance guarantee. Citations: - Section 2B.11 Guidance §1 (p.78) - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD does not specify this information. A correct response should state that the requested value or requirement cannot be determined from the manual rather than inventing an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Table 2A-1, the octagon is used exclusively for the Stop (R1-1) sign, the circle is used exclusively for the Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1) sign, and the pennant (an isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal, pointed right) is used exclusively for the No Passing Zone (W14-3) sign. These shape-to-sign assignments are mandatory, and the exclusive shapes should not be obscured by other signs mounted on the back of the same assembly. Standards (mandatory): - "Particular shapes, as shown in Table 2A-1, shall be used exclusively for specific signs or series of signs, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or class of signs." Table 2A-1 assigns: "Octagon* Stop (R1-1)** ... Circle* Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1)** Pennant (Isosceles Triangle with longer axis horizontal, pointed right)* No Passing Zone (W14-3)**" - Per the table's footnotes: "* This shape shall be limited exclusively to the sign(s) indicated. ** This sign shall be exclusively the shape shown." — so all three shapes are exclusive to their signs, and each of those signs must use its designated shape. Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." Recommended alternatives include installing signs on separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] shows Table 2A-1, "Use of Sign Shapes," listing the octagon paired with Stop (R1-1), the circle paired with Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and the pennant paired with No Passing Zone (W14-3), each marked with the exclusivity footnotes. - [Image 4] shows Section 2C.53, which reinforces the pennant assignment: the NO PASSING ZONE (W14-3) sign shall be a pennant-shaped isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal and pointing to the right. Citations: - Section 2A.05 Standard §1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Guidance §3 (p.42) - Table 2A-1 (p.42) - Page 231

**Gold answer:** Octagon: STOP; circle: Grade Crossing Advance Warning; pennant: NO PASSING ZONE.

In addition, footnote 4 for this row requires a minimum sign contrast ratio of ≥ 3:1, computed as white retroreflectivity divided by red retroreflectivity. Agencies are required to use an assessment or management method designed to maintain signs at or above these levels. Guidance (recommended): - "Except for those signs specifically identified in Paragraph 5 of this Section, one or more of the methods described in 'Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity,' (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), FHWA, or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5. Signs that are identified through the agency's method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Table 2A-5 lists for White on Red: "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7," with Additional Criteria footnote 4: "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)." - Per Table 2A-5 footnote 1, "The minimum maintained retroreflectivity levels shown in this table are in units of cd/lx/m2 measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of -4.0°." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, in which the "White on Red" row lists W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7 spanning all beaded and prismatic sheeting columns, with footnote 4 in the Additional Criteria column specifying the minimum 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §3 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Option §4 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** White at least 35 cd/lx/m², red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and a white-to-red contrast ratio of at least 3:1.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, page 760, p.760, Page 801. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. | At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinction lies in the expectation of a human driver fallback. At Level 3, the Automated Driving System (ADS) performs all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene." At Level 4, the ADS continues to perform the Dynamic Driving Task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — that is, no human fallback is needed. This information is descriptive (Support/table content), not a normative Standard, Guidance, or Option. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, where Level 3 is described as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," while Level 4 is "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task, even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene"; both levels fall under the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" automation category. [Image 2] shows the same Table 5A-1 in the context of page 760, following the Section 5A.03 definitions (including ADS, which specifically describes Level 3, 4, or 5 systems). Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Page 801

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For an expressway or freeway, Table 6B-1 recommends an A dimension of 1,000 feet, a B dimension of 1,500 feet, and a C dimension of 2,640 feet. Table 6B-1 contains information regarding the spacing of advance warning signs. Where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign to the transition and activity areas should be placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways." - "Typical distances for placement of advance warning signs on freeways and expressways should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow. Therefore, the advance warning sign placement should extend on these facilities as far as ½ mile or more." - "The distances contained in Table 6B-1 are approximate, are intended for guidance purposes only, and should be applied with engineering judgment. These distances should be adjusted for field conditions, if necessary, by increasing or decreasing the recommended distances." Options (permitted): - The table text accompanying Section 6B.05 reproduces the values: "Expressway / Freeway 1,000 feet 1,500 feet 2,640 feet," with the footnote explaining that "The A dimension is the distance from the transition or point of restriction to the first sign.
